rray timeline

Benchmarking a single run iterator struct and R_alloc() stride buffers

Codex · Oct 2, 2026 · 4 prompts · 19 replies · #112

Oct 2, 2026, 14:42 UTC

Davis

Let’s talk about the fact that we have 2 structs. One for plan and one for iterator.

This has felt a bit overkill to me

Ive always wanted just rray_run_iterator as the only struct - it seems like it really just need to move the full n v_strides from plan in to take the place of v_stride, and v_dimensions and dimensionality would come over too.

The problem is that we’ve never been able to get performance right. As you can see in the plan/iterator.md, the struct MUST be defined locally to be able to be fast!

But at this point it feels like it might be possible? We probably would have to pass a number of arguments through to the typed variants, but then maybe we’d JUST make the iterator in there? No plan?

What do you think? And we’ll need to do some serious checks for performance

Codex

I’ll trace how the plan and iterator are built, then check the performance notes and benchmark setup. That should tell us whether one local struct can keep the compiler optimizations this code needs.

const xs = ALL_TOOLS.filter(x => /read|edit|write/i.test(x.name + " " + x.description) && /file|fil… 
const xs = ALL_TOOLS.filter(x => /read|edit|write/i.test(x.name + " " + x.description) && /file|filesystem/i.test(x.description)); text(xs);

{"cmd":"sed -n '1,380p' src/strided-iterator2.h","result":{"status":"fulfilled","value":{"chunk_id":"af610a","wall_time_seconds":0.189068084,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":3000,"output":"#ifndef RRAY_STRIDED_ITERATOR2_H\n#define RRAY_STRIDED_ITERATOR2_H\n\n#include \"dimensionality.h\"\n#include \"rlang.h\"\n#include \"size.h\"\n\n#include \"decl/strided-iterator2-decl.h\"\n\n// Strided iterator plan\n//\n// Optimizes and assists in walking a multidimensional point space defined by\n// `v_dimensions`. As it walks, it updates a user provided `start` in an\n// alternate subspace defined by `v_strides`.\n//\n// For performance and flexibility, the user is responsible for managing the run\n// loop along the first axis. We've tried many alternative approaches but they\n// tend to tank performance quickly as you increase the level of abstractions.\n//\n// The plan holds only immutable state. The positions that change as it walks\n// are managed by the caller. This lets the compiler keep these positions in\n// registers, which has significant performance implications.\n//\n// --------------------------------------------------------------------------\n// Examples\n//\n// For broadcasting, the dimensions you broadcast to make up the larger point\n// space. This is walked in order. The original dimensions of the array make\n// up the subspace. So as you walk the output's point space you can create\n// `location`s back into your original array to pull from.\n//\n// For reducing, it's actually a special form of broadcasting. The original\n// dimensions of the array are the point space. The reduced dimensions are the\n// subspace. So as you walk the original array, you can create `location`s into\n// the output to accumulate the reduced result at.\n//\n// For permuting axes, the permuted dimensions make up the point space. The\n// original dimensions of the array make up the subspace. So as you walk the\n// output's point space you can create `location`s back into your original\n// array to pull from.\n//\n// --------------------------------------------------------------------------\n// Optimization - First axis runs\n//\n// After coalescing, the iterator walks the entire first axis in one inner run\n// while holding all later axes fixed. It only updates the later point\n// coordinates between runs, rather than checking and carrying them after every\n// element. This gives the compiler a small loop where the index and locations\n// advance by fixed strides, making it much easier to optimize and vectorize.\n//\n// Practical examples of when this is useful:\n//\n// - Identically shaped binary array operations. Adding two [2, 4, 5] arrays\n//   coalesces to dimensions [40], so the entire operation is one first axis run\n//   where both input locations advance contiguously.\n//\n// - Broadcasting over a later axis. Broadcasting a [2, 3] array to [2, 3, 4]\n//   coalesces to dimensions [6, 4] with location strides [1, 0]. The iterator\n//   performs four first axis runs of size 6, copying six contiguous values\n//   before updating the later axis.\n//\n// - Reducing over a later axis. Reducing a [2, 3, 4] array over its third axis\n//   also coalesces to dimensions [6, 4] with output strides [1, 0]. Each first\n//   axis run accumulates one contiguous slice into six output locations before\n//   advancing along the reduced axis.\n//\n// --------------------------------------------------------------------------\n// Optimization - Coalescing\n//\n// Coalescing axes is an important optimization used to reduce the number of\n// axes we have to iterate over by \"merging\" adjacent compatible ones. This can\n// improve performance by 3-5x on its own in some cases.\n//\n// Practical examples of when this is useful:\n//\n// - Identically shaped binary array operations. Adding two arrays with\n//   dimensions [2, 4, 5] gives both inputs strides [1, 2, 8]. All adjacent axes\n//   coalesce (2 * 1 = 2, 4 * 2 = 8) into one dimension [40] with stride [1],\n//   resulting in one flat vectorizable loop.\n//\n// - Scalar broadcasting across an entire array. Adding a scalar to a [2, 4, 5]\n//   array gives the array strides [1, 2, 8] and the scalar strides [0, 0, 0].\n//   Both coalesce completely, producing a [40] loop with array stride [1] and\n//   scalar stride [0].\n//\n// - Contiguous adjacent axes within a broadcast operation. Broadcasting a\n//   [2, 3] array over point dimensions [2, 3, 4] produces location strides\n//   [1, 2, 0]. The adjacent axes 1 and 2 coalesce (2 * 1 = 2) into size 6,\n//   leaving dimensions [6, 4] and strides [1, 0].\n//\n// - Contiguous regions within a reduction. Reducing a [2, 3, 4] array over its\n//   third axis maps into a [2, 3] result using location strides [1, 2, 0].\n//   Coalescing produces dimensions [6, 4], making it the same as the broadcast\n//   example above.\n//\n// - Point-space dimensions of size 1. Output point dimensions of [1, 3, 4] can\n//   have strides [0, 1, 3]. The dimension 1 first axis is absorbed into the\n//   dimension 3 second axis, adopting stride 1, after which the dimension 4\n//   third axis also coalesces. The result is one dimension [12] with stride\n//   [1]. Note that this isn't broadcasting. This is when both input and output\n//   have an axis that stays dimension 1, which is somewhat rare.\n//\n// For iterator2, note that both sets of location strides must be coalescible,\n// as coalescing changes the output dimensionality, so it's all or nothing.\n//\n// --------------------------------------------------------------------------\n// Optimization - Fixed zero stride paths\n//\n// After coalescing, the first axis is walked by the inner loop. A stride of 0\n// on this axis means that a subspace location stays fixed while the point space\n// moves along it. Broadcasting uses this to reuse an input value. Reducing uses\n// it to accumulate into the same output location. A zero stride on a later axis\n// does not use this path because later axes advance between inner runs.\n//\n// When the inner loop receives the stride as a runtime value, the compiler\n// can't prove that the location is fixed and falls back to a scalar loop. The\n// public iteration macros check for a zero stride and pass a literal 0 to a\n// specialized path. This lets the compiler see that the location does not\n// change. For binary operations, it can then hoist the fixed load out of the\n// loop and vectorize the remaining stride 1 work.\n//\n// Practical examples of when this is useful:\n//\n// - Scalar broadcasting across an entire array. Adding a scalar to a [2, 4, 5]\n//   array coalesces to point dimensions [40], with array strides [1] and scalar\n//   strides [0]. The scalar location stays fixed while the array and output\n//   advance contiguously. The same path is used by arithmetic, comparison,\n//   equality, and extrema operations.\n//\n// - Row broadcasting within a matrix. Adding a [1, 4] row to a [2, 4] array\n//   gives the array strides [1, 2] and row strides [0, 1]. Each inner run\n//   therefore reuses one row value while the array and output advance\n//   contiguously.\n//\n// - Higher dimensional row broadcasting. Adding a [1, 3, 4] array to a\n//   [2, 3, 4] array coalesces to dimensions [2, 12], with array strides [1, 2]\n//   and broadcast strides [0, 1]. Each of the 12 inner runs uses the fixed\n//   path.\n//\n// - Shared leading dimensions of size 1. Adding [1, 1, 4] to [1, 3, 4]\n//   absorbs the shared first axis and produces dimensions [3, 4], with first\n//   input strides [0, 1] and second input strides [1, 3].\n//\n// - Reducing over the first axis. Reducing a [2, 3, 4] array to [1, 3, 4]\n//   produces output strides [0, 1, 3], which coalesce to dimensions [2, 12]\n//   with output strides [0, 1]. Each inner run accumulates into one fixed\n//   output location.\nstruct rray_run_iterator {\n  r_ssize start;\n  r_ssize end;\n  r_ssize v_loc[RRAY_MAX_INPUTS];\n\n  r_ssize size;\n  r_ssize v_point[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];\n\n  // Since coalescing can multiply two axes' dimensions together, we use an\n  // `r_ssize` here even though an individual dimension can't be above an `int`.\n  r_ssize v_dimensions[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];\n  int dimensionality;\n\n  // Strides for all `n` arrays, laid out axis-major in a flat array as\n  // [dimensionality][n], allowing contiguous access when doing \"next\" calls.\n  r_ssize v_strides[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY * RRAY_MAX_INPUTS];\n};\n\nstatic inline struct rray_run_iterator rray_run_iterator(\n  const int* v_dimensions,\n  int dimensionality,\n  const r_ssize* v_strides,\n  r_ssize n\n) {\n  check_dimensionality(dimensionality);\n\n  if (n < 1 || n > RRAY_MAX_INPUTS) {\n    r_stop_internal(\n      \"`n` (%\" R_PRI_SSIZE \") must be between 1 and %d.\",\n      n,\n      RRAY_MAX_INPUTS\n    );\n  }\n\n  struct rray_run_iterator it;\n\n  it.size = rray_size_from_dimensions_checked(\n    v_dimensions,\n    dimensionality,\n    r_lazy_null\n  );\n\n  for (int axis = 0; axis < dimensionality; ++axis) {\n    it.v_dimensions[axis] = (r_ssize) v_dimensions[axis];\n  }\n\n  r_memcpy(\n    it.v_strides,\n    v_strides,\n    sizeof(r_ssize) * (size_t) (dimensionality * n)\n  );\n\n  it.dimensionality = rray__run_iterator_axes_coalesce(\n    it.v_dimensions,\n    it.v_strides,\n    n,\n    dimensionality\n  );\n\n  it.start = 0;\n  it.end = it.v_dimensions[0];\n\n  for (r_ssize i = 0; i < n; ++i) {\n    it.v_loc[i] = 0;\n  }\n\n  r_memset(it.v_point, 0, sizeof(r_ssize) * (size_t) it.dimensionality);\n\n  return it;\n}\n\nstatic inline r_ssize rray_run_iterator_size(\n  const struct rray_run_iterator* it\n) {\n  return it->size;\n}\n\nstatic inline r_ssize rray_run_iterator_start(\n  const struct rray_run_iterator* it\n) {\n  return it->start;\n}\n\nstatic inline r_ssize rray_run_iterator_end(\n  const struct rray_run_iterator* it\n) {\n  return it->end;\n}\n\nstatic inline r_ssize rray_run_iterator_loc(\n  const struct rray_run_iterator* it,\n  r_ssize i\n) {\n  return it->v_loc[i];\n}\n\nstatic inline r_ssize rray_run_iterator_stride(\n  const struct rray_run_iterator* it,\n  r_ssize i\n) {\n  return it->v_strides[i];\n}\n\nstatic inline bool rray_run_iterator_done(const struct rray_run_iterator* it) {\n  return it->start == it->size;\n}\n\nstatic inline void rray_run_iterator_next(\n  struct rray_run_iterator* it,\n  r_ssize n\n) {\n  for (int axis = 1; Each operation passes in a one-element kernel (`ONE`). Anything an operation needs beyond that one-element kernel, like a 64-bit total or a pairwise sum, has to become a new macro parameter or a new macro. `plans/accuracy.md` shows how quickly that grows.\n\nThis plan looks at the alternative: an iterator that the operation drives itself, with the loops written in each function. We built three iterator designs into a variant of rray4 and benchmarked them against the current macros on broadcasting, addition and sums.\n\n---\n\n# Summary\n\n- A per-element iterator (a `next()` that hands back `x` and `out` locations for every element) is simplest to use, but too slow. It's up to 7x slower on sums, 2x to 2.7x slower on double addition, and 1.1x to 1.5x slower on broadcasting.\n\n- An iterator that hands back one run at a time, used with a single loop over the run, matches the macros when every operand moves along the run. When one operand stays fixed, it's 1.3x to 1.5x slower on broadcasting and addition, and 4x to 7x slower on sums.\n\n- The same run iterator, with each function writing one loop per stride case (an operand fixed or moving), matches the macros in nearly every case we measured. The exception is very short runs: adding `[10, 1e6] + [10, 1]` (a million runs of 10 elements) is 4% to 6% slower, in every one of six runs. It's faster in one case: summing `[10, 1e6]` over axis 1 takes 2.75 ms against 3.53 ms, because the total stays in a local variable.\n\n- That design is what NumPy does. It is also what the macros already do. The loops are the same, so the speed is the same. What changes is where the loops live: in each function, where they can be read and changed, instead of inside a macro.\n\n- One run iterator can serve any number of inputs, replacing today's separate one-input and two-input plans. It's as fast as the specialized ones, as long as each function passes the number of inputs as a literal (`1`, `2`) and the call is inlined. With the count known only at runtime, short runs get 25% to 75% slower.\n\n- The short-run gap goes away when the iterator does less per run: keep one location array that the iterator advances directly (no per-run copy for the caller), and advance at the end of each run instead of checking for the first run at the start, like the macros do. That version is within 2.5% of the macro on every short-run case.\n\n- Keep the plan and the iterator as separate structs. A merged struct is just as fast when it's built inside the kernel. But when the driver builds it and the kernel uses it through a pointer, raw broadcasting gets up to 5x slower and a strided mean 11% slower. With a small `const` plan passed in and the iterator built locally in the kernel, the fast version is the default, with no rule to remember.\n\n- The cost is repetition. rray4 has 84 `RRAY_BINARY()` calls, 32 `RRAY_REDUCE()` calls and 5 `RRAY_BROADCAST_ATOMIC()` calls. Writing every stride case out by hand in each of them is a lot of code. How to handle the per-type repetition is the main open question.\n\nRecommendation: adopt one run iterator for any number of inputs, in the \"advance at the end\" shape, with per-stride-case loops written in each function. Decide separately how to avoid writing every type out by hand.\n\n---\n\n# How it works today\n\n`src/strided-iterator.h` builds a plan from a point space (the dimensions being walked) and a subspace (the strides of the array being read or written). After merging axes that can be walked as one (\"coalescing\"), the first axis is walked as one run, and later axes advance between runs with `RRAY_STRIDED_ITERATOR_NEXT()`.\n\nThe header documents three things that matter here:\n\n- \"For performance and flexibility, the user is responsible for managing the run loop along the first axis. We've tried many alternative approaches but they tend to tank performance quickly as you increase the level of abstractions.\"\n\n- The plan holds only fixed state. The positions that change are kept by the caller, so the compiler can keep them in registers.\n\n- When a stride along the run is 0, the macros take a separate path where the compiler can see the location doesn't change. It then loads the fixed value once and vectorizes the rest.\n\nEach macro owns the full walk: allocate the output, set up the plan, loop over runs, branch on the stride cases, call `ONE` per element, and advance between runs. A few functions (`combine.c`, `index.c`, `permute-axes.c`, `split.c`) already write their own loops against the plan directly, without a macro.\n\n---\n\n# What NumPy does\n\nThis section is from knowledge of NumPy's source, not checked against it. NumPy isn't installed on the benchmark machine.\n\n- One iterator, `nditer`, for everything. It reorders axes so the inner loop follows memory order, merges axes that can be walked as one, and hands out one run at a time: a pointer, a length, and a stride for each operand. For reductions, it walks the input in memory order no matter which axis is reduced.\n\n- Reductions reuse the elementwise kernel. `np.add.reduce` calls the same inner loop as `np.add`, with operands `(out, in, out)`. When a whole run feeds one output, the output's stride is 0.\n\n- Each typed inner loop checks its strides and picks a path. The `float64` add loop first checks `IS_BINARY_REDUCE` (output stride 0, output is also the first input). If so, it runs a pairwise sum over the run into one local total. Otherwise it picks a fast path for contiguous operands, a fixed scalar operand, and so on, and uses SIMD there.\n\n- SIMD is written by hand with NumPy's own layer (and Highway for newer kernels), compiled for several CPU levels and picked when NumPy loads. rray4 can only rely on the compiler vectorizing at R's default `-O2`, since CRAN doesn't allow `-march=native`.\n\n- When data needs a type conversion or is misaligned, `nditer` copies it in chunks (8192 elements by default) into a contiguous buffer, and the inner loop runs on that.\n\n- The typed inner loops are generated, not written by hand: once from `.c.src` templates expanded by a Python script, now mostly C++ templates. There is one loop body per operation, expanded across types. The iterator never knows what operation it's running, and the inner loop never knows how many dimensions the array has.\n\nSo NumPy uses the \"run iterator, per-stride-case loops\" design, and avoids writing every type out by generating the per-type loops.\n\n---\n\n# The iterators\n\nBoth iterators wrap an existing `rray_strided_iterator_plan` (one subspace) or `rray_strided_iterator2_plan` (two subspaces). axis < dimensionality; ++axis) {\n    plan.v_dimensions[axis] = (r_ssize) v_dimensions[axis];\n  }\n\n  r_memcpy(\n    plan.v_strides,\n    v_strides,\n    sizeof(r_ssize) * (size_t) (dimensionality * n)\n  );\n\n  plan.dimensionality = rray__run_plan_axes_coalesce(\n    plan.v_dimensions,\n    plan.v_strides,\n    n,\n    dimensionality\n  );\n\n  return plan;\n}\n\nstatic inline r_ssize rray_run_plan_size(const struct rray_run_plan* plan) {\n  return plan->size;\n}\n\nstruct rray_run_iterator {\n  r_ssize start;\n  r_ssize end;\n  r_ssize v_loc[RRAY_MAX_INPUTS];\n  r_ssize v_stride[RRAY_MAX_INPUTS];\n\n  r_ssize size;\n  r_ssize v_point[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];\n};\n\nstatic inline struct rray_run_iterator rray_run_iterator(\n  const struct rray_run_plan* plan,\n  r_ssize n\n) {\n  struct rray_run_iterator it;\n\n  it.size = plan->size;\n\n  it.start = 0;\n  it.end = plan->v_dimensions[0];\n\n  for (r_ssize i = 0; i < n; ++i) {\n      if (!rray__run_plan_axes_coalescible(\n            left_dimension,\n            v_left_strides[i],\n            right_dimension,\n            v_right_strides[i]\n          )) {\n        coalescible = false;\n        break;\n      }\n    }\n\n    if (coalescible) {\n      if (left_dimension == 1) {\n        for (r_ssize i = 0; i < n; ++i) {\n        v_out_strides[i] = v_right_strides[i];\n      }\n    }\n  }\n\n  return out_axis + 1;\n}\n\nstatic inline bool rray__run_plan_axes_coalescible(\n  r_ssize left_dimension,\n  r_ssize left_stride,\n  r_ssize right_dimension,\n  r_ssize right_stride\n) {\n  return left_dimension == 1 || right_dimension == 1 ||\n    right_stride == left_dimension * left_stride;\n}\n\n#endif\n"}}}

11:- A per-element iterator (a `next()` that hands back `x` and `out` locations for every element) is simplest to use, but too slow. It's up to 7x slower on sums, 2x to 2.7x slower on double addition, and 1.1x to 1.5x slower on broadcasting.
13:- An iterator that hands back one run at a time, used with a single loop over the run, matches the macros when every operand moves along the run. When one operand stays fixed, it's 1.3x to 1.5x slower on broadcasting and addition, and 4x to 7x slower on sums.
15:- The same run iterator, with each function writing one loop per stride case (an operand fixed or moving), matches the macros in nearly every case we measured. The exception is very short runs: adding `[10, 1e6] + [10, 1]` (a million runs of 10 elements) is 4% to 6% slower, in every one of six runs. It's faster in one case: summing `[10, 1e6]` over axis 1 takes 2.75 ms against 3.53 ms, because the total stays in a local variable.
19:- One run iterator can serve any number of inputs, replacing today's separate one-input and two-input plans. It's as fast as the specialized ones, as long as each function passes the number of inputs as a literal (`1`, `2`) and the call is inlined. With the count known only at runtime, short runs get 25% to 75% slower.
21:- The short-run gap goes away when the iterator does less per run: keep one location array that the iterator advances directly (no per-run copy for the caller), and advance at the end of each run instead of checking for the first run at the start, like the macros do. That version is within 2.5% of the macro on every short-run case.
23:- Keep the plan and the iterator as separate structs. A merged struct is just as fast when it's built inside the kernel. But when the driver builds it and the kernel uses it through a pointer, raw broadcasting gets up to 5x slower and a strided mean 11% slower. With a small `const` plan passed in and the iterator built locally in the kernel, the fast version is the default, with no rule to remember.
39:- The plan holds only fixed state. The positions that change are kept by the caller, so the compiler can keep them in registers.
41:- When a stride along the run is 0, the macros take a separate path where the compiler can see the location doesn't change. It then loads the fixed value once and vectorizes the rest.
55:- Each typed inner loop checks its strides and picks a path. The `float64` add loop first checks `IS_BINARY_REDUCE` (output stride 0, output is also the first input). If so, it runs a pairwise sum over the run into one local total. Otherwise it picks a fast path for contiguous operands, a fixed scalar operand, and so on, and uses SIMD there.
57:- SIMD is written by hand with NumPy's own layer (and Highway for newer kernels), compiled for several CPU levels and picked when NumPy loads. rray4 can only rely on the compiler vectorizing at R's default `-O2`, since CRAN doesn't allow `-march=native`.

Because `next()` is `static inline` and the struct never leaves the function, the compiler keeps its fields in registers, so the header's concern about positions in registers doesn't apply. That held for every case below, with Apple clang 17. GCC is not checked.
137:`next()` advances one element and hands back `index` (in the point space) and `loc` (in the subspace). 700:- `merged_reset`: one merged iterator, reset for each output.

728:- For a single walk, merged and separate are the same within this session's noise (about 3%).
730:- Copying the plan for every walk costs 4% to 7% when there are a million outputs. Resetting one merged iterator avoids that copy and matches the separate plan.
732:This round only shows that merging costs nothing inside one function. A real merged design wouldn't build the struct in the kernel, though. The driver would build it and hand it over.
734:## Round 2: merged, built by the driver
736:The drivers build the merged struct and pass a pointer to the kernel. The kernels live in their own file and are marked `noinline`, matching today's function-pointer call from driver to kernel. Three kernel versions:
738:- `copy`: copies the struct into a local (`struct rray_run_iterator_m it_local = *p_it;`), then runs the same loops as round 1.
742:- `pointer_locals`: uses the pointer, but reads `start`, `end`, the strides and the starting locations into locals at the top of each run.
744:"Local" is round 1's merged struct built inside the kernel. Same timing method as round 1.
746:| Raw broadcast | Macro | Copy | Pointer | Pointer, locals |
752:| Integer mean | Macro | Local | Copy | Pointer | Pointer, locals |
761:| Double | Macro | Local | Copy | Pointer | Pointer, locals |
775:- Working through the pointer is the trap. For raw output, C allows a byte written through `Rbyte*` to change any object, including `it->end` and `it->v_stride[0]`. So the compiler reloads them on every element and can't vectorize: 5.2x slower on `[4000, 1]` to `[4000, 4000]`. Double output isn't affected, because C's type rules let the compiler assume a `double` write can't change an `r_ssize` field. That's why it never showed up in the double tests. The strided mean also lost 11% through the pointer.
777:- Reading each run's fields into locals fixes it everywhere, and was the fastest version on the short-walk means (6.98 ms against 7.87 ms for the macro). But it's a rule every loop has to follow. Forgetting it costs nothing visible on `double` and only bites on raw, or anything else written through a character type.
779:- Copying the struct into a local also avoids the raw problem, but was 30% slower on the short sum and 7% to 10% slower on the short-walk means. The copy happens once per call (about 33 KB, microseconds), so it can't account for milliseconds by itself. A guess is that clang optimizes the loops less well after copying such a large struct. It hasn't been looked into.
781:- The separate plan avoids all of this without a rule. The kernel gets a small `const` plan, which is only read between runs, and builds its changing positions as locals that can't be confused with the output.

## What the numbers say

- The element iterator is slower everywhere. "Did this run just end?" is checked on every element, which stops the compiler from vectorizing. Sums are hit hardest: an output total down a column has to be written to memory and read back on every add.

- The element iterator costs least on integer addition (about 6%), because the overflow check already stops that loop from vectorizing.

- The run iterator with one loop is as fast as the macro whenever every operand moves along the run. When an operand is fixed, it loses: up to 1.5x on addition, 1.3x on broadcasting, and 4x to 7x on sums. With a runtime stride, the compiler can't tell the location never moves, so it reloads the fixed value (or re-stores the total) on every element.

- The run iterator with one loop per stride case is within run-to-run noise of the macro nearly everywhere, because it is the same set of loops. The exception is addition with very short runs. Across six runs of the suite, `[10, 1e6] + [10, 1]` took 7.56 to 7.92 ms with the macro and 8.03 to 8.43 ms with the run iterator (integer: 10.97 to 11.29 ms against 11.56 to 11.81 ms). With a million runs of 10 elements, the iterator's per-run work shows up, most likely the `it->end != 0` check and copying the run's starting locations. It hasn't been looked into further. Summing `[10, 1e6]` over axis 1 is faster (2.75 ms against 3.53 ms): its total lives in a local variable, while the macro reads and writes `v_out[out_loc]` on each add. Runs here are only 10 elements long, so that matters more.

- Broadcasting and addition are mostly limited by writing a 128 MB output, so the differences between designs are smaller there than for sums, which write almost nothing.

- An unexpected result: for integer addition with a fixed operand, the specialized loop is about 20% slower than the plain loop (19.4 ms against 16.3 ms for `[4000, 4000] + [1, 4000]`). This affects the current macro too, not just the new design. It hasn't been looked into. A guess is that clang compiles the overflow check differently once one side is a known fixed value. Checked operations could skip the fixed-operand cases, which would also make them shorter.

---

# Any number of inputs

Today there are three plans: `rray_strided_iterator_plan` (one input), `rray_strided_iterator2_plan` (two inputs) and `rray_strided_iterator_n_plan` (any number). `src/index.c` drives the last one through a `switch` with `case 1:` to `case 4:`, passing the count to `RRAY_STRIDED_ITERATOR_NEXT_N()` as a literal so the compiler unrolls its loops over inputs.

## What NumPy does

From knowledge of `nditer_templ.c.src` and `nditer_api.c`, not checked against the source.

- `nditer` is written once for any number of operands. A template generates specialized copies of its `next()` for 1 operand, 2 operands, and any number (crossed with 1, 2, or any number of dimensions, and with its flags). `NpyIter_GetIterNext()` picks one and returns a function pointer. That is the same idea as rray4's `case 1:` to `case 4:`, generated instead of written by hand.

- Ufuncs call `next()` once per run, not per element, so the generic per-operand loops only run between runs.

- The per-element loop belongs to the ufunc, which always knows its operand count (a binary ufunc has 2 inputs and 1 output). It reads `args[0]`, `args[1]` and `args[2]` directly and never loops over operands.

## `rray_run_iterator_n`

The same as `rray_run_iterator`, with arrays of per-input locations and strides, and the number of inputs passed to both functions. When the caller passes a literal and the functions are inlined, the loops over `n` unroll away.

```c
#define RRAY_MAX_INPUTS 8

struct rray_run_iterator_n {
  r_ssize start;
  r_ssize end;
  r_ssize v_loc[RRAY_MAX_INPUTS];
  r_ssize v_stride[RRAY_MAX_INPUTS];

  r_ssize size;
  r_ssize run_size;
  r_ssize v_loc_start[RRAY_MAX_INPUTS];
  const struct rray_strided_iterator_n_plan* plan;
  r_ssize v_point[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];

# Plan and iterator: separate or merged

Every iterator above keeps two structs: an immutable plan (dimensions, strides, size), built once by the driver and passed to the kernel as a `const` pointer, and an iterator holding the changing positions, built inside the kernel. This section checks whether that split matters, or whether one merged struct would do.

## How the variant package is wired

- Every timing goes through the real entry points (`rray_broadcast()`, `rray_add()`, `rray_sum()`, `rray_mean()`) and their unchanged drivers (`rray_broadcast()` itself, `rray_binary_arithmetic()`, `rray_reduce()`, `rray_reduce_nested()`).

- Each driver already builds the plan, picks the per-type kernel, and calls it through a function pointer with `const plan*`. Every iterator version above is built inside that kernel, as a local, from the plan pointer it already receives. Its address only goes to `static inline` functions (`done()`, `next()`), so it never leaves the kernel, and the compiler can keep its positions in registers. No extra parameters needed threading through.

## The merged struct

```c
struct rray_run_iterator_m {
  r_ssize start;
  r_ssize end;
  r_ssize v_loc[RRAY_MAX_INPUTS];
  r_ssize v_stride[RRAY_MAX_INPUTS];

  r_ssize size;
  r_ssize run_size;
  int dimensionality;
  r_ssize v_dimensions[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
  r_ssize v_strides[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY * RRAY_MAX_INPUTS];
  r_ssize v_point[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
};

static inline void rray_run_iterator_m_reset(
  struct rray_run_iterator_m* it,
  const r_ssize n
) {
  it->start = 0;
  it->end = it->run_size;

  for (r_ssize i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
    it->v_loc[i] = 0;
  }

  r_memset(it->v_point, 0, sizeof(r_ssize) * (size_t) it->dimensionality);
}
```

`rray_run_iterator_m_init()` copies the dimensions and strides from a plan, then calls `reset()`. `done()` and `next()` are the same as the `advance` iterator, with the plan fields read from the struct itself. With room for 64 dimensions and 64 inputs, the stride array alone is 32 KB.

## Round 1: merged, built inside the kernel

The kernel builds the merged struct itself, copying from the plan the driver passed in. For the nested integer mean (one inner walk per output), three ways to handle the inner walk:

- `advance`: a new separate-plan iterator for each output.

- `merged_reset`: one merged iterator, reset for each output.

- `merged_fresh`: a new merged iterator for each output, copying the plan every time.

The integer mean makes a single pass, so the per-walk setup cost isn't hidden behind a second pass. Median of 3 rounds of 30 iterations, with the order of the versions rotated between rounds.

| Case | Macro | Advance | Merged |
|---|---|---|---|
| Broadcast `[10, 1]` to `[10, 1e6]` | 7.10 | 7.10 | 7.01 |
| Broadcast `[1, 1e6]` to `[10, 1e6]` | 6.84 | 7.23 | 7.13 |
| Add `[10, 1e6] + [10, 1]` | 8.38 | 8.40 | 8.52 |
| Add `[10, 1e6] + [1, 1e6]` | 7.89 | 7.88 | 7.95 |
| Add, integer, `[10, 1e6] + [10, 1]` | 12.00 | 11.84 | 12.00 |
| Add `[4000, 4000] + [4000, 4000]` | 10.89 | 10.54 | 10.71 |
| Sum `[10, 1e6]` over 1 | 3.68 | 2.88 | 3.05 |
| Sum `[10, 1e6]` over 2 | 4.01 | 3.92 | 3.98 |
| Sum `[4000, 4000]` over 1 | 13.80 | 13.80 | 13.78 |
| Sum `[4000, 4000]` over 2 | 2.44 | 2.42 | 2.40 |

| Integer mean | Outputs | Macro | Advance | Merged, reset | Merged, fresh |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `[4000, 4000]` over 1 | 4000 | 14.16 | 14.17 | 14.15 | 14.15 |
| `[4000, 4000]` over 2 | 4000 | 43.25 | 42.84 | 41.65 | 43.01 |
| `[10, 1e6]` over 1 | 1e6 | 8.17 | 7.95 | 7.76 | 8.45 |
| `[1e6, 10]` over 2 | 1e6 | 8.16 | 7.91 | 7.87 | 8.51 |
| `[200, 200, 200]` over 1, 3 | 200 | 7.60 | 7.58 | 7.73 | 7.63 |
| `[10, 1e5, 10]` over 1, 3 | 1e5 | 6.61 | 6.50 | 6.54 | 6.64 |

- For a single walk, merged and separate are the same within this session's noise (about 3%).

- Copying the plan for every walk costs 4% to 7% when there are a million outputs. Resetting one merged iterator avoids that copy and matches the separate plan.

This round only shows that merging costs nothing inside one function. A real merged design wouldn't build the struct in the kernel, though. The driver would build it and hand it over.

## Round 2: merged, built by the driver

The drivers build the merged struct and pass a pointer to the kernel. The kernels live in their own file and are marked `noinline`, matching today's function-pointer call from driver to kernel. Three kernel versions:

- `copy`: copies the struct into a local (`struct rray_run_iterator_m it_local = *p_it;`), then runs the same loops as round 1.

- `pointer`: uses the driver's struct through the pointer, with the same loop text (`it->end`, `it->v_stride[0]`, ...).

- `pointer_locals`: uses the pointer, but reads `start`, `end`, the strides and the starting locations into locals at the top of each run.

"Local" is round 1's merged struct built inside the kernel. Same timing method as round 1.

| Raw broadcast | Macro | Copy | Pointer | Pointer, locals |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `[10, 1]` to `[10, 1e6]` | 3.35 | 2.86 | 5.47 | 3.16 |
| `[1, 1e6]` to `[10, 1e6]` | 3.44 | 3.22 | 4.80 | 3.13 |
| `[4000, 1]` to `[4000, 4000]` | 1.23 | 1.25 | 6.46 | 1.43 |

| Integer mean | Macro | Local | Copy | Pointer | Pointer, locals |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `[4000, 4000]` over 1 | 14.12 | 14.15 | 14.15 | 14.15 | 14.16 |
| `[4000, 4000]` over 2 | 42.77 | 42.82 | 43.00 | 47.71 | 43.12 |
| `[10, 1e6]` over 1 | 7.92 | 7.88 | 8.44 | 7.26 | 6.99 |
| `[1e6, 10]` over 2 | 7.87 | 7.87 | 8.45 | 7.26 | 6.98 |
| `[200, 200, 200]` over 1, 3 | 7.67 | 7.59 | 7.93 | 7.60 | 7.64 |
| `[10, 1e5, 10]` over 1, 3 | 6.60 | 6.51 | 7.17 | 7.20 | 7.09 |

| Double | Macro | Local | Copy | Pointer | Pointer, locals |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Broadcast `[10, 1]` to `[10, 1e6]` | 7.12 | 7.30 | 6.79 | 6.87 | 7.16 |
| Broadcast `[1, 1e6]` to `[10, 1e6]` | 7.15 | 7.16 | 7.02 | 7.14 | 7.03 |
| Add `[10, 1e6] + [10, 1]` | 8.24 | 8.49 | 8.25 | 8.44 | 8.67 |
| Add `[10, 1e6] + [1, 1e6]` | 8.04 | 7.88 | 7.73 | 7.71 | 7.65 |
| Add `[4000, 4000] + [4000, 4000]` | 10.63 | 10.90 | 10.61 | 10.48 | 10.70 |
| Sum `[10, 1e6]` over 1 | 3.90 | 2.76 | 3.60 | 2.89 | 2.72 |
| Sum `[10, 1e6]` over 2 | 3.99 | 3.94 | 3.93 | 3.99 | 3.92 |
| Sum `[4000, 4000]` over 1 | 13.78 | 13.78 | 13.76 | 13.78 | 13.79 |
| Sum `[4000, 4000]` over 2 | 2.41 | 2.39 | 2.41 | 2.43 | 2.42 |

## What the numbers say

- Working through the pointer is the trap. For raw output, C allows a byte written through `Rbyte*` to change any object, including `it->end` and `it->v_stride[0]`. So the compiler reloads them on every element and can't vectorize: 5.2x slower on `[4000, 1]` to `[4000, 4000]`. Double output isn't affected, because C's type rules let the compiler assume a `double` write can't change an `r_ssize` field. That's why it never showed up in the double tests. The strided mean also lost 11% through the pointer.

- Reading each run's fields into locals fixes it everywhere, and was the fastest version on the short-walk means (6.98 ms against 7.87 ms for the macro). But it's a rule every loop has to follow. Forgetting it costs nothing visible on `double` and only bites on raw, or anything else written through a character type.

- Copying the struct into a local also avoids the raw problem, but was 30% slower on the short sum and 7% to 10% slower on the short-walk means. The copy happens once per call (about 33 KB, microseconds), so it can't account for milliseconds by itself. A guess is that clang optimizes the loops less well after copying such a large struct. It hasn't been looked into.

- The separate plan avoids all of this without a rule. The kernel gets a small `const` plan, which is only read between runs, and builds its changing positions as locals that can't be confused with the output.

So keep the plan and the iterator separate. The reason isn't that merging is slower in itself. It's that the split makes the fast version the default. The header comment in `src/strided-iterator.h` should give that reason instead of "positions managed by the caller stay in registers", since round 1 shows an iterator struct keeps them in registers just as well.

For the nested reducer, the inner iterator should get a `reset()` that puts it back at the start of the plan, with a new starting location, instead of building a new iterator for every output.

PKG_CPPFLAGS = -I./rlang

benchmark_library <- Sys.getenv("RRAY_BENCH_LIBRARY", unset = NA_character_)

if (!is.na(benchmark_library)) {
  .libPaths(c(benchmark_library, .libPaths()))
}

devtools::load_all(quiet = TRUE)

if (!requireNamespace("broadcast", quietly = TRUE)) {
  stop("The broadcast package must be installed.", call. = FALSE)
}

iterations <- 30L

benchmark_pair <- function(group, case, rray4, comparison, other) {
  rray4()
  other()
  gc()

  result <- bench::mark(
    rray4 = rray4(),
    comparison = other(),
    iterations = iterations,
    check = TRUE,
    memory = TRUE
  )

  data.frame(
    group = group,
    case = case,
    implementation = c("rray4", comparison),
    median_ms = as.numeric(result$median) * 1000,
    mem_alloc_mb = as.numeric(result$mem_alloc) / 1024^2,
    relative_time = as.numeric(result$median) / min(as.numeric(result$median)),
    row.names = NULL
  )
}

bind_results <- function(...) {
  do.call(rbind, list(...))
}

n <- 1e6L
x <- array(seq(1, 2, length.out = n), c(1000L, 1000L))
y <- array(seq(2, 3, length.out = n), c(1000L, 1000L))
scalar <- array(2, c(1L, 1L))
row <- array(seq(2, 3, length.out = 1000L), c(1L, 1000L))
column <- array(seq(2, 3, length.out = 1000L), c(1000L, 1L))
alternating_x <- array(
  seq(1, 2, length.out = 1000L),
  c(1L, 10L, 1L, 10L, 1L, 10L)
)
alternating_y <- array(
  seq(2, 3, length.out = 1000L),
  c(10L, 1L, 10L, 1L, 10L, 1L)

Thinking

Preparing benchmark design

Planning performance checks

Thinking

Preparing benchmark design

Planning performance checks

Reviewing benchmark coverage

I think one rray_run_iterator is viable if each typed kernel constructs it as a local variable. Passing the dimensions and strides into that kernel is the key change. The earlier benchmarks found that a merged struct built locally performed about the same as the separate plan and iterator. The serious slowdown came when the driver built it and the kernel worked through its pointer: raw broadcasting was up to 5.2× slower, and one strided mean case was 11% slower (plans/iterators.md).

The current uncommitted broadcast changes follow the promising shape: the typed function receives dimensions and strides, then builds its own iterator. I left those files untouched. I would keep the input count literal (1 or 2) at each typed call, since the earlier short-run tests found a 25% to 75% cost when that count was known only at runtime.

Before adopting it across the package, I’d compare isolated builds of the current code and the completed one-struct version, with identical inputs and alternating benchmark order. The checks that matter most are raw broadcast (the earlier 5× failure), million-run shapes with runs of 10 elements, zero-stride addition, both reduction directions, nested mean with many outputs, and tiny arrays where setup cost shows. I’d check results for equality before timing and inspect compiler vectorization for any repeatable regression. The existing bench/iterator.R has useful shapes, but it does not by itself compare two isolated builds.

So I agree with trying the simpler design. The prior result rules out passing the merged iterator by pointer, not constructing it locally in the typed kernel. I haven’t run fresh timings while the other agent’s version is still changing.

Thinking

Preparing the benchmark script

Constructing benchmark script

Thinking

Preparing the benchmark script

Constructing benchmark script

Both copies installed successfully with the same Apple clang 17 -O2 flags. The snapshot changes only broadcast and its iterator header, so I’m timing broadcast now. Raw arrays and short runs are the cases most likely to expose the earlier slowdown.

Thinking

Creating reusable benchmark code

Bash 
const root=load("bench_root");
const code=`.libPaths(c(Sys.getenv("RRAY_BENCH_LIB"), .libPaths()))
library(rray4)
mk_raw <- function(n) as.raw((seq_len(n) - 1L) %% 251L)
raw_col <- array(mk_raw(4000L), c(4000L, 1L))
raw_row <- array(mk_raw(4000L), c(1L, 4000L))
dbl_col <- array(seq_len(4000L) * 0.5, c(4000L, 1L))
dbl_row <- array(seq_len(4000L) * 0.5, c(1L, 4000L))
raw_short_col <- array(mk_raw(10L), c(10L, 1L))
raw_short_row <- array(mk_raw(1000000L), c(1L, 1000000L))
chr_col <- array(as.character(seq_len(1000L)), c(1000L, 1L))
list_col <- array(as.list(seq_len(1000L)), c(1000L, 1L))
alternating_raw <- array(mk_raw(1000L), c(1L, 10L, 1L, 10L, 1L, 10L))
tiny <- array(as.raw(7L), 1L)
cases <- list(
  raw_column_16m = function() rray_broadcast(raw_col, c(4000L, 4000L)),
  raw_row_16m = function() rray_broadcast(raw_row, c(4000L, 4000L)),
  raw_scalar_16m = function() rray_broadcast(tiny, c(4000L, 4000L)),
  raw_short_fixed = function() rray_broadcast(raw_short_row, c(10L, 1000000L)),
  raw_short_moving = function() rray_broadcast(raw_short_col, c(10L, 1000000L)),
  raw_alternating = function() rray_broadcast(alternating_raw, rep(10L, 6L)),
  double_column_16m = function() rray_broadcast(dbl_col, c(4000L, 4000L)),
  double_row_16m = function() rray_broadcast(dbl_row, c(4000L, 4000L)),
  character_column_1m = function() rray_broadcast(chr_col, c(1000L, 1000L)),
  list_column_1m = function() rray_broadcast(list_col, c(1000L, 1000L)),
  tiny_1 = function() rray_broadcast(tiny, 1L),
  tiny_10 = function() rray_broadcast(tiny, 10L),
  tiny_100 = function() rray_broadcast(tiny, 100L),
  tiny_1000 = function() rray_broadcast(tiny, 1000L)
)
cat("case,median_us,hash\\n")
for (name in names(cases)) {
  f <- cases[[name]]
  output <- f()
  hash <- digest::digest(output, algo = "xxhash64")
  gc()
  iterations <- if (startsWith(name, "tiny_")) 200L else 30L
  b <- bench::mark(f(), iterations = iterations, check = FALSE, memory = FALSE)
  cat(name, sprintf("%.3f", as.numeric(b$median) * 1e6), hash, sep = ",")
  cat("\\n")
  flush.console()
}`;
store("bench_code",code);
const cmd=`RRAY_BENCH_LIB='${root}/lib-baseline' Rscript -e '${code}'`;
const r=await tools.exec_command({cmd,max_output_tokens:2500,yield_time_ms:30000});text(r);if(r.session_id)store("bench_session",r.session_id);

// @exec: {"yield_time_ms": 30000, "max_output_tokens": 3500}
const root=load("bench_root"),code=load("bench_code");let rows=[];for(let round=1;round<=3;round++){for(const variant of (round%2?["baseline","candidate"]:["candidate","baseline"])){const r=await tools.exec_command({cmd:`RRAY_BENCH_LIB='${root}/lib-${variant}' Rscript -e '${code}'`,max_output_tokens:2000,yield_time_ms:30000});if(r.exit_code!==0){text(JSON.stringify({round,variant,result:r}));exit()}const data=r.output.trim().split("\n").slice(1).map(s=>{const [name,us,hash]=s.split(",");return {round,variant,name,us:Number(us),hash}});rows.push(...data);text(JSON.stringify({round,variant,rows:data.map(x=>[x.name,x.us])}));}}store("bench_rows",rows);

The first paired runs show matching output hashes for every case. Most timings are close, but list output is repeatedly slower in the one-struct copy. Large allocations vary a lot between runs, so I’m repeating the suspect cases with more samples before drawing a conclusion.

Bash

// @exec: {"yield_time_ms": 30000, "max_output_tokens": 1800}
const root=load("bench_root");
const code=`mk_raw <- function(n) as.raw((seq_len(n) - 1L) %% 251L)
raw_col <- array(mk_raw(4000L), c(4000L, 1L))
raw_row <- array(mk_raw(4000L), c(1L, 4000L))
short_col <- array(mk_raw(10L), c(10L, 1L))
short_row <- array(mk_raw(1000000L), c(1L, 1000000L))
dbl_col <- array(seq_len(4000L) * 0.5, c(4000L, 1L))
list_col <- array(as.list(seq_len(1000L)), c(1000L, 1L))
chr_col <- array(as.character(seq_len(1000L)), c(1000L, 1L))
scalar <- array(as.raw(7L), 1L)
cases <- list(
  raw_column = function() rray_broadcast(raw_col, c(4000L, 4000L)),
  raw_row = function() rray_broadcast(raw_row, c(4000L, 4000L)),
  raw_scalar = function() rray_broadcast(scalar, c(4000L, 4000L)),
  raw_short_fixed = function() rray_broadcast(short_row, c(10L, 1000000L)),
  raw_short_moving = function() rray_broadcast(short_col, c(10L, 1000000L)),
  double_column = function() rray_broadcast(dbl_col, c(4000L, 4000L)),
  list_column = function() rray_broadcast(list_col, c(1000L, 1000L)),
  character_column = function() rray_broadcast(chr_col, c(1000L, 1000L))
)
libs <- c(baseline = Sys.getenv("RRAY_BENCH_BASE"), candidate = Sys.getenv("RRAY_BENCH_CAND"))
rows <- list()
for (round in seq_len(4L)) {
  for (name in names(cases)) {
    variants <- if ((round + match(name, names(cases))) %% 2L == 0L) c("baseline", "candidate") else c("candidate", "baseline")
    f <- cases[[name]]
    for (variant in variants) {
      library(rray4, lib.loc = libs[[variant]], character.only = TRUE)
      hash <- digest::digest(f(), algo = "xxhash64")
      gc()
      iterations <- if (name %in% c("double_column", "list_column")) 40L else 80L
      b <- bench::mark(f(), iterations = iterations, check = FALSE, memory = FALSE)
      rows[[length(rows) + 1L]] <- data.frame(round = round, case = name, variant = variant, median_us = as.numeric(b$median) * 1e6, hash = hash)
      detach("package:rray4", unload = TRUE)
    }
  }
}
out <- do.call(rbind, rows)
stopifnot(all(vapply(split(out$hash, out$case), function(x) length(unique(x)) == 1L, logical(1))))
write.csv(out, Sys.getenv("RRAY_BENCH_OUT"), row.names = FALSE)
for (name in names(cases)) {
  x <- out[out$case == name, ]
  base <- median(x$median_us[x$variant == "baseline"])
  cand <- median(x$median_us[x$variant == "candidate"])
  cat(sprintf("%s baseline %.2f us candidate %.2f us ratio %.3f\\n", name, base, cand, cand / base))
}`;
store("paired_bench_code",code);
const cmd=`RRAY_BENCH_BASE='${root}/lib-baseline' RRAY_BENCH_CAND='${root}/lib-candidate' RRAY_BENCH_OUT='${root}/paired.csv' Rscript -e '${code}'`;
const r=await tools.exec_command({cmd,max_output_tokens:1800,yield_time_ms:30000});text(r);if(r.session_id)store("paired_session",r.session_id);
Script completed
Wall time 0.5 seconds

Preparing short benchmark

Checking compiler vectorization

A paired run in one R process removed the large allocation swings. It shows no repeatable list slowdown and no raw broadcast cliff. The one possible loss is about 3% to 4% on a million short runs where the input moves along each run. I’m checking that case, its fixed-input counterpart, and small calls more closely.

Bash 
// @exec: {"yield_time_ms": 30000, "max_output_tokens": 1500}
const root=load("bench_root");
const code=`mk_raw <- function(n) as.raw((seq_len(n) - 1L) %% 251L)
raw_short_col <- array(mk_raw(10L), c(10L, 1L))
raw_short_row <- array(mk_raw(1000000L), c(1L, 1000000L))
dbl_short_col <- array(as.double(seq_len(10L)), c(10L, 1L))
dbl_short_row <- array(as.double(seq_len(1000000L)), c(1L, 1000000L))
raw_scalar <- array(as.raw(7L), 1L)
dbl_scalar <- array(7, 1L)
cases <- list(
  raw_short_moving = function() rray_broadcast(raw_short_col, c(10L, 1000000L)),
  raw_short_fixed = function() rray_broadcast(raw_short_row, c(10L, 1000000L)),
  double_short_moving = function() rray_broadcast(dbl_short_col, c(10L, 1000000L)),
  double_short_fixed = function() rray_broadcast(dbl_short_row, c(10L, 1000000L)),
  tiny_raw_10 = function() rray_broadcast(raw_scalar, 10L),
  tiny_raw_1000 = function() rray_broadcast(raw_scalar, 1000L),
  tiny_double_10 = function() rray_broadcast(dbl_scalar, 10L),
  tiny_double_1000 = function() rray_broadcast(dbl_scalar, 1000L)
)
libs <- c(baseline = Sys.getenv("RRAY_BENCH_BASE"), candidate = Sys.getenv("RRAY_BENCH_CAND"))
rows <- list()
for (round in seq_len(6L)) {
  for (name in names(cases)) {
    variants <- if ((round + match(name, names(cases))) %% 2L == 0L) c("baseline", "candidate") else c("candidate", "baseline")
    f <- cases[[name]]
    for (variant in variants) {
      library(rray4, lib.loc = libs[[variant]])
      hash <- digest::digest(f(), algo = "xxhash64")
      gc()
      iterations <- if (startsWith(name, "tiny")) 500L else 100L
      b <- bench::mark(f(), iterations = iterations, check = FALSE, memory = FALSE)
      rows[[length(rows) + 1L]] <- data.frame(round = round, case = name, variant = variant, median_us = as.numeric(b$median) * 1e6, hash = hash)
      detach("package:rray4", unload = TRUE)
    }
  }
}
out <- do.call(rbind, rows)
stopifnot(all(vapply(split(out$hash, out$case), function(x) length(unique(x)) == 1L, logical(1))))
write.csv(out, Sys.getenv("RRAY_BENCH_OUT"), row.names = FALSE)
for (name in names(cases)) {
  x <- out[out$case == name & out$round > 1L, ]
  base <- median(x$median_us[x$variant == "baseline"])
  cand <- median(x$median_us[x$variant == "candidate"])
  ratios <- x$median_us[x$variant == "candidate"] / x$median_us[x$variant == "baseline"]
  cat(sprintf("%s baseline %.3f us candidate %.3f us ratio %.3f rounds %s\\n", name, base, cand, cand / base, paste(sprintf("%.3f", ratios), collapse = " ")))
}`;
store("short_bench_code",code);
const r=await tools.exec_command({cmd:`RRAY_BENCH_BASE='${root}/lib-baseline' RRAY_BENCH_CAND='${root}/lib-candidate' RRAY_BENCH_OUT='${root}/short.csv' Rscript -e '${code}'`,max_output_tokens:1500,yield_time_ms:30000});text(r);if(r.session_id)store("short_session",r.session_id);
Script completed

Investigating broadcast regression

Testing restrict aliasing

The tighter short-run check found a real gap: raw [10, 1] broadcast to [10, 1e6] was about 10% slower in the one-struct copy across five measured rounds. The fixed-input shape was essentially equal. The broadcast tests pass in the copied tree (108 expectations), so I’m checking whether keeping the first-axis stride close to the changing iterator state removes that cost.

Testing local restrict variant

Keeping a separate first-axis stride inside the one struct made no measurable difference. I’m testing one more cause in the copied build: whether clang treats raw output writes as possibly changing iterator fields between runs. This will tell us if the short-run gap is tied to pointer aliasing.

Preparing assembly comparison

Checking workspace snapshot

Thinking

Preparing assembly comparison

Checking workspace snapshot

The raw inner loop still vectorizes in both builds. A restrict output pointer also leaves the short-run timing unchanged, so that aliasing idea does not explain the gap. I’m measuring several run lengths at the same total output size to see whether the extra cost is paid once per run.

Thinking

Constructing benchmark inputs

Bash 
// @exec: {"yield_time_ms": 30000, "max_output_tokens": 1200}
const root=load("bench_root");const code=`mk_raw <- function(n) as.raw((seq_len(n) - 1L) %% 251L)
lengths <- c(2L, 5L, 10L, 20L, 50L, 100L, 1000L)
cases <- lapply(lengths, function(run_length) local({
  x <- array(mk_raw(run_length), c(run_length, 1L))
  dimensions <- c(run_length, 10000000L / run_length)
  function() rray_broadcast(x, dimensions)
}))
names(cases) <- paste0("run_", lengths)
libs <- c(baseline = Sys.getenv("RRAY_BENCH_BASE"), candidate = Sys.getenv("RRAY_BENCH_CAND"))
rows <- list()
for (round in seq_len(5L)) {
  for (name in names(cases)) {
    variants <- if ((round + match(name, names(cases))) %% 2L == 0L) c("baseline", "candidate") else c("candidate", "baseline")
    f <- cases[[name]]
    for (variant in variants) {
      library(rray4, lib.loc = libs[[variant]])
      hash <- digest::digest(f(), algo = "xxhash64")
      gc()
      b <- bench::mark(f(), iterations = 50L, check = FALSE, memory = FALSE)
      rows[[length(rows) + 1L]] <- data.frame(round = round, case = name, variant = variant, median_us = as.numeric(b$median) * 1e6, hash = hash)
      detach("package:rray4", unload = TRUE)
    }
  }
}
out <- do.call(rbind, rows)
stopifnot(all(vapply(split(out$hash, out$case), function(x) length(unique(x)) == 1L, logical(1))))
write.csv(out, Sys.getenv("RRAY_BENCH_OUT"), row.names = FALSE)
for (name in names(cases)) {
  x <- out[out$case == name & out$round > 1L, ]
  base <- median(x$median_us[x$variant == "baseline"])
  cand <- median(x$median_us[x$variant == "candidate"])
  cat(sprintf("%s baseline %.1f us candidate %.1f us ratio %.3f\\n", name, base, cand, cand / base))
}`;
store("run_length_code",code);
const r=await tools.exec_command({cmd:`RRAY_BENCH_BASE='${root}/lib-baseline' RRAY_BENCH_CAND='${root}/lib-candidate' RRAY_BENCH_OUT='${root}/run-length.csv' Rscript -e '${code}'`,max_output_tokens:1200,yield_time_ms:30000});text(r);if(r.session_id)store("runlength_session",r.session_id);
Script completed
Wall time 15.6 seconds

run_5 baseline 5400.6 us candidate 5964.1 us ratio 1.104

run_20 baseline 1788.2 us candidate 1815.9 us ratio 1.015
run_50 baseline 903.6 us candidate 964.6 us ratio 1.068
run_100 baseline 1436.3 us candidate 1490.3 us ratio 1.038
run_1000 baseline 171.9 us candidate 174.3 us ratio 1.014

Thinking

Verifying benchmark results

The one-struct version avoids the old 5× raw broadcast slowdown, but I found a repeatable cost on short runs. I timed separate installed builds from HEAD and a snapshot of the current working tree, using Apple clang 17 at -O2. The shared checkout was untouched.

Raw broadcast, 10 million output elements HEAD One struct Change
Moving input, runs of 10 2.95 ms 3.25 ms 10% slower
Fixed input, runs of 10 3.22 ms 3.23 ms About equal
Moving input, runs of 1,000 0.172 ms 0.174 ms About equal

A sweep of short run lengths found roughly 4% to 10% slower results for the moving-input case. Other paired broadcast timings, including list output and tiny calls, were close. Output hashes matched in every timed case, and the copied candidate passed all 108 broadcast test expectations.

Both builds still vectorize the raw inner loop. In separate temporary experiments, copying the first-axis stride into the struct and adding restrict to the output pointer did not remove the short-run cost. That points toward work between runs, but I have not pinned it to an instruction yet.

The timing scripts and results are in the temporary copy, including focused results. This snapshot converts broadcast only, so addition and reductions still need the same comparison once their one-struct versions exist.

Davis

I’m also curious to know if we’d be better off using multiple R_Alloc calls to Allocate iterator field buffers or exactly the size required rather than using a static but rather large buffer. It should be way less than 32kb for 99.9% of cases, and that may help the compiler, not sure

//   produces output strides [0, 1, 3], which coalesce to dimensions [2, 12]
//   with output strides [0, 1]. Each inner run accumulates into one fixed
//   output location.
struct rray_run_iterator {
  r_ssize start;
  r_ssize end;
  r_ssize v_loc[RRAY_MAX_INPUTS];

  r_ssize size;
  r_ssize v_point[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];

  // Since coalescing can multiply two axes' dimensions together, we use an
  // `r_ssize` here even though an individual dimension can't be above an `int`.
  r_ssize v_dimensions[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
  int dimensionality;

  // Strides for all `n` arrays, laid out axis-major in a flat array as
  // [dimensionality][n], allowing contiguous access when doing "next" calls.
  r_ssize v_strides[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY * RRAY_MAX_INPUTS];
};

static inline struct rray_run_iterator rray_run_iterator(
  const int* v_dimensions,
  int dimensionality,
  const r_ssize* v_strides,
  r_ssize n
) {
  check_dimensionality(dimensionality);

  if (n < 1 || n > RRAY_MAX_INPUTS) {
    r_stop_internal(
      "`n` (%" R_PRI_SSIZE ") must be between 1 and %d.",
      n,
      RRAY_MAX_INPUTS
    );
  }

  struct rray_run_iterator it;

  it.size = rray_size_from_dimensions_checked(
    v_dimensions,
    dimensionality,
    r_lazy_null
  );

  for (int axis = 0; axis < dimensionality; ++axis) {
    it.v_dimensions[axis] = (r_ssize) v_dimensions[axis];
  }

  r_memcpy(
    it.v_strides,
    v_strides,
    sizeof(r_ssize) * (size_t) (dimensionality * n)
  );

  it.dimensionality = rray__run_iterator_axes_coalesce(
    it.v_dimensions,
    it.v_strides,
    n,
    dimensionality
  );

  it.start = 0;
  it.end = it.v_dimensions[0];

  for (r_ssize i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
    it.v_loc[i] = 0;
  }

  r_memset(it.v_point, 0, sizeof(r_ssize) * (size_t) it.dimensionality);

  return it;
}

static inline r_ssize rray_run_iterator_size(
  const struct rray_run_iterator* it
) {
  return it->size;
}

static inline r_ssize rray_run_iterator_start(
  const struct rray_run_iterator* it
) {
  return it->start;
}

static inline r_ssize rray_run_iterator_end(
  const struct rray_run_iterator* it
) {
  return it->end;
}

static inline r_ssize rray_run_iterator_loc(
  const struct rray_run_iterator* it,
  r_ssize i
) {
  return it->v_loc[i];
}

static inline r_ssize rray_run_iterator_stride(
  const struct rray_run_iterator* it,
  r_ssize i
) {
  return it->v_strides[i];
}

static inline bool rray_run_iterator_done(const struct rray_run_iterator* it) {
  return it->start == it->size;
}

static inline void rray_run_iterator_next(
  struct rray_run_iterator* it,
  r_ssize n
) {
  for (int axis = 1; axis < it->dimensionality; ++axis) {
    const r_ssize* v_strides = it->v_strides + axis * n;
    const r_ssize dimension = it->v_dimensions[axis];

    ++it->v_point[axis];

    if (it->v_point[axis] < dimension) {
      for (r_ssize i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        it->v_loc[i] += v_strides[i];
      }
      break;
    }

    it->v_point[axis] = 0;

    for (r_ssize i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
      it->v_loc[i] -= (dimension - 1) * v_strides[i];
    }
  }

  it->start = it->end;
  it->end += it->v_dimensions[0];
}

static inline int rray__run_iterator_axes_coalesce(
  r_ssize* v_dimensions,
  r_ssize* v_strides,
  r_ssize n,
  int dimensionality
) {
  int out_axis = 0;

  for (int axis = 1; axis < dimensionality; ++axis) {
    const r_ssize left_dimension = v_dimensions[out_axis];
    r_ssize* v_left_strides = v_strides + out_axis * n;
    const r_ssize right_dimension = v_dimensions[axis];
    const r_ssize* v_right_strides = v_strides + axis * n;

    bool coalescible = true;

    for (r_ssize i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
      if (!rray__run_iterator_axes_coalescible(
            left_dimension,
            v_left_strides[i],
            right_dimension,
            v_right_strides[i]
          )) {
        coalescible = false;
        break;
      }
    }

    if (coalescible) {
      if (left_dimension == 1) {
        for (r_ssize i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
          v_left_strides[i] = v_right_strides[i];
        }
      }
      v_dimensions[out_axis] = left_dimension * right_dimension;
    } else {
      ++out_axis;
      v_dimensions[out_axis] = right_dimension;
      r_ssize* v_out_strides = v_strides + out_axis * n;
      for (r_ssize i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        v_out_strides[i] = v_right_strides[i];
      }
    }
  }

  return out_axis + 1;
}

static inline bool rray__run_iterator_axes_coalescible(

/* Since memory allocated from the heap is non-moving, R_alloc just
   allocates off the heap as RAWSXP/REALSXP and maintains the stack of
   allocations through the ATTRIB pointer.  The stack pointer R_VStack
   is traced by the collector. */
void *vmaxget(void)
{
    return (void *) R_VStack;
}

void vmaxset(const void *ovmax)
{
    R_VStack = (SEXP) ovmax;
}

char *R_alloc(size_t nelem, int eltsize)
{
    R_size_t size = nelem * eltsize;
    /* doubles are a precaution against integer overflow on 32-bit */
    double dsize = (double) nelem * eltsize;
    if (dsize > 0) {
	SEXP s;
#ifdef LONG_VECTOR_SUPPORT
	/* 64-bit platform: previous version used REALSXPs */
	if(dsize > R_XLEN_T_MAX)  /* currently 4096 TB */
	    error(_("cannot allocate memory block of size %0.f %s"),
		  dsize/R_pow_di(1024.0, 4), "Tb");
	s = allocVector(RAWSXP, size + 1);
#else
	if(dsize > R_LEN_T_MAX) /* must be in the Gb range */
	    error(_("cannot allocate memory block of size %0.1f %s"),
		  dsize/R_pow_di(1024.0, 3), "Gb");
	s = allocVector(RAWSXP, size + 1);
#endif
	ATTRIB(s) = R_VStack;
	R_VStack = s;
	return (char *) STDVEC_DATAPTR(s);
    }
    /* One programmer has relied on this, but it is undocumented! */
    else return NULL;
}

R’s local source shows that each R_alloc() call creates a transient R vector. I’ve made two temporary variants: one allocates each field buffer separately, and one allocates a single compact buffer and splits it among fields. I’ll compare their call cost and their run-loop cost.

dbl_col <- array(seq_len(4000L) * 0.5, c(4000L, 1L))
list_col <- array(as.list(seq_len(1000L)), c(1000L, 1L))
alternating <- array(mk_raw(1000L), c(1L, 10L, 1L, 10L, 1L, 10L))
raw_scalar <- array(as.raw(7L), 1L)
dbl_scalar <- array(7, 1L)
high_dim <- array(as.raw(7L), rep(1L, 64L))
high_dim_out <- c(rep(1L, 63L), 10L)
cases <- list(
  raw_short_moving = function() rray_broadcast(raw_short_col, c(10L, 1000000L)),
  raw_short_fixed = function() rray_broadcast(raw_short_row, c(10L, 1000000L)),
  raw_column = function() rray_broadcast(raw_col, c(4000L, 4000L)),
  double_column = function() rray_broadcast(dbl_col, c(4000L, 4000L)),
  list_column = function() rray_broadcast(list_col, c(1000L, 1000L)),
  alternating = function() rray_broadcast(alternating, rep(10L, 6L)),
  tiny_raw_10 = function() rray_broadcast(raw_scalar, 10L),
  tiny_raw_1000 = function() rray_broadcast(raw_scalar, 1000L),
  tiny_double_10 = function() rray_broadcast(dbl_scalar, 10L),
  high_dim_tiny = function() rray_broadcast(high_dim, high_dim_out)
)
libs <- c(stack = Sys.getenv("RRAY_BENCH_STACK"), heap1 = Sys.getenv("RRAY_BENCH_HEAP1"), heap4 = Sys.getenv("RRAY_BENCH_HEAP4"))
rows <- list()
for (round in seq_len(5L)) {
  for (case_index in seq_along(cases)) {
    name <- names(cases)[[case_index]]
    f <- cases[[case_index]]
    variants <- names(libs)
    variants <- variants[((seq_along(variants) + round + case_index - 2L) %% length(variants)) + 1L]
    for (variant in variants) {
      library(rray4, lib.loc = libs[[variant]])
      hash <- digest::digest(f(), algo = "xxhash64")
      gc()
      iterations <- if (startsWith(name, "tiny") || name == "high_dim_tiny") 500L else 50L
      b <- bench::mark(f(), iterations = iterations, check = FALSE, memory = FALSE)
      rows[[length(rows) + 1L]] <- data.frame(round = round, case = name, variant = variant, median_us = as.numeric(b$median) * 1e6, hash = hash)
      detach("package:rray4", unload = TRUE)
    }
  }
}
out <- do.call(rbind, rows)
stopifnot(all(vapply(split(out$hash, out$case), function(x) length(unique(x)) == 1L, logical(1))))
write.csv(out, Sys.getenv("RRAY_BENCH_OUT"), row.names = FALSE)
for (name in names(cases)) {
  x <- out[out$case == name & out$round > 1L, ]
  medians <- vapply(names(libs), function(v) median(x$median_us[x$variant == v]), numeric(1))
  cat(sprintf("%s stack %.3f heap1 %.3f heap4 %.3f ratios %.3f %.3f\\n", name, medians[[1]], medians[[2]], medians[[3]], medians[[2]] / medians[[1]], medians[[3]] / medians[[1]]))
}`;
store("heap_bench_code",code);const r=await tools.exec_command({cmd:`RRAY_BENCH_STACK='${root}/lib-candidate' RRAY_BENCH_HEAP1='${root}/lib-heap1' RRAY_BENCH_HEAP4='${root}/lib-heap4' RRAY_BENCH_OUT='${root}/heap.csv' Rscript -e '${code}'`,max_output_tokens:1800,yield_time_ms:30000});text(r);if(r.session_id)store("heap_session",r.session_id);

Redesigning iterator initialization

Patching hybrid iterator

The exact-sized R_alloc() versions did not speed up broadcast. One allocation was close to the fixed array on most ordinary shapes; four allocations were a little worse. Both were slower on a tiny 64-axis case. I’ll test a small inline stride buffer next, with a dynamic fallback for more than two inputs, to see whether reducing the struct itself helps.

The small inline buffer did not help the hot paths. It avoids an R allocation for the common one-input case, but the short moving-input run is about 5% slower than the current fixed array in repeated paired timings. The single compact R_alloc() buffer is closer to current speed. I’m checking correctness for all three variants, then I’ll summarize the tradeoff.

Considering VLA buffers

The first result suggests one compact allocation is the best of the heap options, but that version also moved the small position fields off the stack. I’m isolating the large stride field now: the iterator will keep its position fields local and allocate only dimensionality * n strides.

Calculating struct sizes

Comparing allocation footprints

Allocating only strides gives a much smaller local struct and stayed close to current speed in the paired runs, but it did not show a clear speed gain. I’m making one final hybrid variant that accesses its inline strides directly for one or two inputs. That avoids the pointer access in the earlier inline test while keeping a dynamic fallback for larger input counts.

Bash

struct rray_run_iterator {
  r_ssize start;
  r_ssize end;
  r_ssize v_loc[RRAY_MAX_INPUTS];

  r_ssize size;
  r_ssize v_point[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];

  // Since coalescing can multiply two axes' dimensions together, we use an
  // `r_ssize` here even though an individual dimension can't be above an `int`.
  r_ssize v_dimensions[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
  int dimensionality;

  // Strides for all `n` arrays, laid out axis-major in a flat array as
  // [dimensionality][n], allowing contiguous access when doing "next" calls.
  r_ssize v_strides[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY * 2];
  r_ssize* v_extra_strides;
};

static inline struct rray_run_iterator rray_run_iterator(
  const int* v_dimensions,
  int dimensionality,
  const r_ssize* v_strides,
  r_ssize n
) {
  check_dimensionality(dimensionality);

  if (n < 1 || n > RRAY_MAX_INPUTS) {
    r_stop_internal(
      "`n` (%" R_PRI_SSIZE ") must be between 1 and %d.",
      n,
      RRAY_MAX_INPUTS
    );
  }

  struct rray_run_iterator it;

  it.v_extra_strides = n <= 2
    ? NULL
    : (r_ssize*) R_alloc(
        (size_t) (dimensionality * n),
        sizeof(r_ssize)
      );
  r_ssize* v_it_strides = n <= 2 ? it.v_strides : it.v_extra_strides;

  it.size = rray_size_from_dimensions_checked(
    v_dimensions,
    dimensionality,
    r_lazy_null
  );

  for (int axis = 0; axis < dimensionality; ++axis) {
    it.v_dimensions[axis] = (r_ssize) v_dimensions[axis];
  }

  r_memcpy(
    v_it_strides,
    v_strides,
    sizeof(r_ssize) * (size_t) (dimensionality * n)
  );

  it.dimensionality = rray__run_iterator_axes_coalesce(
    it.v_dimensions,
    v_it_strides,
    n,
    dimensionality
  );

  it.start = 0;
  it.end = it.v_dimensions[0];

  for (r_ssize i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
    it.v_loc[i] = 0;
  }

  r_memset(it.v_point, 0, sizeof(r_ssize) * (size_t) it.dimensionality);

  return it;
}

static inline r_ssize rray_run_iterator_size(
  const struct rray_run_iterator* it
) {
  return it->size;
}

static inline r_ssize rray_run_iterator_start(
  const struct rray_run_iterator* it
) {
  return it->start;
}

static inline r_ssize rray_run_iterator_end(
  const struct rray_run_iterator* it
) {
  return it->end;
}

static inline r_ssize rray_run_iterator_loc(
  const struct rray_run_iterator* it,
  r_ssize i
) {
  return it->v_loc[i];
}

static inline r_ssize rray_run_iterator_stride(
  const struct rray_run_iterator* it,
  r_ssize i,
  r_ssize n
) {
  return n <= 2 ? it->v_strides[i] : it->v_extra_strides[i];
}

static inline bool rray_run_iterator_done(const struct rray_run_iterator* it) {
  return it->start == it->size;
}

static inline void rray_run_iterator_next(
  struct rray_run_iterator* it,
  r_ssize n
) {
  const r_ssize* v_it_strides =
    n <= 2 ? it->v_strides : it->v_extra_strides;

Evaluating inline threshold

Checking R_alloc semantics

I tested this in isolated copies. Smaller buffers did not make broadcast faster, but one layout cut the local iterator from about 34 KB to 2.6 KB without a measured loss on the main short-run case.

Layout R_alloc() calls for one input Result
Current fixed arrays 0 Reference
Four exact-sized field buffers 4 Similar on ordinary shapes; 26% slower on a tiny 64-axis case
One packed field buffer 1 Similar on ordinary shapes; about 20% slower on that 64-axis case
Allocate strides only 1 Similar on ordinary shapes; about 9% slower on that case
Inline strides for up to two inputs, dynamic fallback 0 Matched the measured short-run and 64-axis cases

The last version keeps direct access to the inline strides. Its raw inner loop still vectorizes at the same width. All temporary variants passed the 108 broadcast expectations. The paired results and test variant are available to inspect.

I’d avoid multiple R_alloc() calls per iterator. Each call creates a transient R vector, and repeated construction inside a reduction would retain those allocations until the R call ends. If reducing stack use is the goal, the small inline buffer looks promising. These timings exercise one input; the two-input path and dynamic fallback still need checks before using it across the package. I made no changes to the shared checkout.
